The beginning of the study is marked with the recitation of the prior santi (TĀ 4, 1; transl. van Buitenen, Pravargya:63-64). The two sāntis, the prior and the posterior, are also recited at the beginning and end of each performance (according to BSS, VaikhŚS, KŚS also at the beginning and end of the collection of the materials).

2 The word *ṛddhiparimitam* is explained by S on this place as *sādhanadravya-samṛddhyā parito* nirmitam praudham, modifying *sátram*. But his explanation in a similar phrase in TB 2, 2, 8, 4 implies a very improbable connection with *yaṣas* in yáśaskāmāḥ. *dhānādisamṛddhyā yuktam yaśaḥ* kāmayamānāḥ*.* My rendering of the term corresponds to B's *kālam yavat* *phalasiddhir bhavati tāvantam kālam*. This is a very reasonable inter-pretation which fits TB 2, 2, 8, 4 as well.

3 Introduction, note 69.

4 ny *akāmayata*, “desired... for himself." This place confirms Caland's remark that this word means "wollte für sich allein behalten" (Caland, "*Wunschopfer*', Amsterdam 1908:25, note 90).

5 (a) In the SB version it is Viṣṇu (and not Makha Vaiṣṇava) who simply takes his bow and three arrows. We may translate makhó víṣṇur in SB 14, 1.1, 1 with "Visnu, the Sacrifice," instead of suggesting the presence of two different gods as in Eggeling's translation "Makha, Viṣṇu.” makhó víṣnur corresponds to Makha Vaiṣṇava in the TĀ-version. SB 14, 1, 1, 13 has that “Makha (the Sacrifice), indeed, is the same as Viṣṇu.”

(b) Read *yajñájanma* instead of yajilájanmā? All editions have the latter form without variant readings, but *iṣudhanvám (púnyajanma*) is neuter sg.

6 upadíka here and in TB and SB 14, 1, 1, 8, is a certain species of ants that is supposed to find water even in the desert. Zysk, Religious Healing, interprets *upajika* in AV 2, 3, 4 and 6,100, 2 as termites or white ants that take up water, and the two words, *upadika* and upajíka, probably refer to the same species of ants. Their connection with a bow may be taken as a *jeu de* mots*: dhánvan* means "bow" and "desert, dry place"; cf. AV 6, 100, 2ab: *yád vo devá* upajīkā *ásiñcan* dhánvany udakám. For the *upadika/*upajika see also Krick, *Feuergründung:* 143, note 372. In the Kaṭha version it is Indra who in the form of the ants eats through the bowstring (KaṭhĀ 3, 207+),

7 The loss of life-energy *(vīryà*) is connected with the loss of the head. So, with the head, the life-energy flew off. Rather than: "Virile energy (or seed *vīryam)* fell from the mighty one *(mahat)*” (Muir, quoted in the introduction of TĀ edition C, p. 27). S and Bh, commenting upon TĀ 5, 3, 4, explain that *vīryà* resides, in the context of that passage, in

[[P106]]

the face (múkha): S: "in the body of a man, the *vīryà*, the ability to see, hear etc., . . . is found in the face." Bh: "the *vīryà* corresponds to the face."

8 *gráha*, "ladleful" is a technical term of the Soma sacrifice. It occurs in its proper context in the similar TS passage 6, 4, 9. It is not adapted to the new context of the Pravargya, in which no real "ladlefuls" are offered from the Gharma milk, either because the word was taken in the secondary meaning of “libation” (Renou, *Vocabulaire du Rituel Védique* (Paris 1954): 62f), or in order to create an additional connection with the Soma sacrifice, the high status of which was well established.

### Anuvāka 2

9 [(a) The oblation is offered by the Adhvaryu with the verse TĀ 4, 2, 1(1) on the Āhavanīya fire. The verse = TS 1, 2, 13a (where it forms part of the recitation at *the havirdhāna*-*maṇḍapa-karaṇa,* Caland-Henry, Agniṣṭoma:80-81 and comes *after* the Pravargya in case the Agniṣṭoma comprises this rite) = TS 4, 1, *1d* (where it forms part of the recitation at the *ukhā*-*sambharana* of the Agnicayana, a rite parallel in several respects to the *pravargya*-*sambharaṇa*) = ŚvetU 2, 4 = RV 5, 81, 1.

*yuñjáte* mána utá *yuñjáte dhíyah*  
*viprā viprasya* bṛható *vípaścítaḥ*  
ví hótrā dadhe vayunāvíd éka ít  
mahí *devásya* savitúḥ *páristutiḥ*,

> "*They* yoke *their* minds, *and* yoke *the visions, the inspired (priests yoke the* visions) of the mighty *inspired (Savitṛ*). He alone, who knows *the* implications, establishes the *sacrificial* acts*;* great is the *praise* of *the god Savitṛ.”*

For discussions and translations of the verse see especially Gonda, Vision:115f; and Renou, *ÉVP* 15 (1966): 24. The Kaṭhas, who have the verse too, comment at this place: "With mind verily Prajāpati performed the sacrifice, then with speech and then with action. With mind the sacrificer performs the sacrifice, then with speech, then with action."

(b) This interesting verse and its place at the beginning of this and some other rites clearly indicate the importance of bringing the mind in the right condition before or at the beginning of the performance (Cf. Gonda, Vision:36-40.) The verb *yuj-*, “yoke,” is in its concrete sense used in connection with horses or other animals yoked to a vehicle. Here, the implication seems to be that with mind and visions or hymns *(dhíyah)* as horses, the chariot of the sacrifice should traverse some space. This space is according to B. Oguibenine the space between “phénomènes de divers ordres" ("phenomena of different orders") between which connections and identifications should be established. The 'connections and identifications should be established "entre le microcosme et le macrocosme, entre le monde rituel et le monde mythique, entre l'ordre humain et divin." ("between the microcosm and the macrocosm, between the ritual world and the mythical world, between the human order and the divine order"; B. Oguibenine, "Sur le terme

[[P107]]

*yoga,* le verbe *yuj-* et quelques-uns de leurs dérivés dans les hymnes védiques,” *IIJ* *27/2:* especially p. 86,87*)*.

10 This section is also in TS 5, 1, 1. S connects the puronuvākya with the gayatri, the yājyā with the tristubh, the devatā with the jagati and the vaṣatkāra with the anustubh. We often find that the anustubh-metre represents a fourth, concluding element after the triad gāyatrī, triṣṭubh, jagati. Therefore, the connection of the anustubh with the vaṣaṇkāra, the concluding part of the formulas that accompany a *juhoti*-oblation, is understandable. The place of the devata in the triad corresponding to gayatri, triṣṭubh and jagati, however, is somewhat peculiar. For, the name of the devatā, the deity to whom the oblation and the accompanying verses are directed, is uttered *before* the puronuvākya (by the Adhvaryu) and *before* the yājyā by the Hotṛ in the words *ye³ yajāmahe* (plus the name of the devatā in the accusative). It is not a separate formula that is uttered like the other three. See Caland, *'Wunschopfer*":XIII-XIV and Hillebrandt, Ritualliteratur:99.

11 *hotavyàm* dikṣitásya *gṛhấ3/4í*, *ná hotavyấ3m íti*. This phrase occurs, in a different context, in TS 6, 1, 4, 5 (referring to TS 1, 2, 3b, whispered by the sacrificer before drinking the vrata-milk). The dikṣitá cannot mean the one who has undergone the Dīkṣā of the normal Agniṣṭoma: the collection of the materials for the Pravargya that starts with the Savitr-oblation and that is discussed here, takes place at the *adhyavasānam* (Caland- Henry, *Agniṣṭoma*:§12), i.e. before the Dīkṣaṇīyeṣṭi etc. *(Agniṣtoma*:§15ff). According to S, there are some ritualists who place the *pravargya*-*sambharana* not before the Dīkṣaṇīyeṣṭi, but after the completion of the Dīkṣā. The Śrauta Sūtras mention this possibility for someone who undergoes the Dīkṣā at another place than the place of sacrifice (cf. Caland, note 1 and 2 on ĀpŚS 15, 1, 2). So our passage may refer to this probably exceptional situation.

However, there is another possibility. The word *dikṣitá* may refer to a sacrificer who has undergone the Avāntaradīkṣā of the Pravargya and in that case the Avāntaradīkṣā is not obligatory because the sacrificer who is *dikṣitá* at this point is mentioned as a special case. We have *dikṣitá* referring to someone who has undergone the Avāntaradīkṣā also in KaṭhĀ 198+. This was already suggested by van Buitenen *(Pravargya*:55, note 2), who referred to BSS 9, 1. This contains the following passage: "If the prior *santi* has not been (already) gone through, it should be gone through at this juncture. Then the Adhvaryu should melt clarified butter over the Gārhapatya fire, purify it, take four spoonfuls of it into the ladle, and, while the sacrificer remains touching him, offer on the Āhavanīya fire an oblation to Savitṛ with '*they yoke their mind and* they *yoke the* visions etc.". In the case of a sacrificer who has not been initiated, the Adhvaryu should offer an oblation with the above-mentioned verse; in the case of a sacrificer who has been initiated, he should (only) murmur that verse" (translation Dandekar, Śrautakośa II, Engl.part 1:28-29, my translation of the *pratīka*, van Buitenen translates anvārabdhe *yajamāne* erroneously with "in case the yajamana is only latterly initiated"). Now the *santi* can only refer to the santi recitation of the Pravargya. It is prescribed both in the Pravargya proper (in ĀpŚS at a

[[P108]]

later stage: ĀpŚS 15, 5, 4), and in the Avāntaradīkṣā, (BSS 9, 19; ĀpŚS 15, 20, 2). If it has been already gone through here, at the very beginning of the Pravargya proper, before the collection of the materials, it has most probably made part of a form of the Pravargya Avāntaradīkṣā, preceding the performance and to be undergone by the sacrificer (see Introduction section 3.3 and note 130 below). But this form of the Pravargya Avāntaradīkṣā is not explicitly discussed in the Śrauta Sūtras. By the time the Pravargya was completely incorporated in the Agniṣṭoma, the Avāntaradīkṣā for the sacrificer became redundant and was eventually dropped. But there are still some reminiscences of it in this book of the Āraṇyaka.

12 *havír val dikṣitáḥ*, "the oblation is actually the initiate": nominal sentence, as in *yajñó vai* makháḥ "Makha is actually the Sacrifice" and, in a typical anaphoric construction, Síro vá *etád* yajñásya, yát *pravargyaḥ,* lit.: "That is actually the head of the Sacrifice: the Pravargya." Many complete identifications (A=B) may actually be short expressions for: there exists a special relation (*bándhu)* between A and B (note 142 below). These identifications and connections, which may often strike us as strange and bizarre, are perhaps better appreciated when we assume that the Vedic sacrifice is a complex consisting of actions, words and thoughts, that express and explain an original experience of unity or interconnection between different spheres of existence (Cf. B. Oguibenine in II 27/2:92).

13 "He should say only the yajus-formula." See Caland note 2 on ApŚS 15, 1, 2. Here, in the Āraṇyaka, two possibilities for a sacrificer who has already been initiated (see note 11 above), are given consideration: a) the Adhvaryu-priest performs the complete oblation (according to the Śrauta Sūtras consisting in the putting on the fire of a thorny fire-stick, followed by the four-fold butter oblation with the verse "*they* yoke *their* mind..."); b) the verse is recited without butter oblation, and probably also without putting the fire-stick on the fire. In ĀpŚS, the two possibilities that are mentioned for a sacrificer who has already been initiated, are somewhat different: a) the recitation of the verse accompanies the putting on the fire of the thorny fire-stick only; b) the recitation of the verse takes place without butter oblation and without putting the fire-stick on the fire.

14 In TS 2, 6, 2, 5, the *vasat*-call is a *vájra* (see note 16 below) that can be hurled at the foe. See also Krick, Feuergründung:555. note 1504.

15 This passage corresponds to TS 6, 2, 10, 1.

16 The comparison of the shovel with a *vajra* supports Rau's conclusions about the shape of a *vájra* in Vedic texts. It must have been a kind of harpoon. See Rau, *Metallgeräte*:37-46.

17 Appeasement may have been thought necessary simply because "the shovel is like a *vájra*.*"* The author of the Āraṇyaka has probably interpreted *nārī* like S: “you are na *ari*, not an enemy." Otherwise there would have been no basis for him to state that the mantra produced "appeasement." *ábhri* may have been derived from the rare root *bhri*, “to injure,” thus: "not injuring.”

18 [[P109]] This half-verse is used by the Adhvaryu to invite the Brahmán priest to rise and to go to the clay-pit (east of the Āhavanīya). The other half of this verse (TĀ 4, 2, 1/2(4), identical with RV 1, 40, 1) is uttered by the Brahmán and Adhvaryu priest together, after the Brahman priest has risen: *úpa prá* yantu marútaḥ *sudánavaḥ*  
*indra* prāsúr *bhavā sácā*, “Let *the very liberal* Maruts come here. *Indra, be quickly* at *hand*." There is another use of this verse, according to which the Adhvaryu is addressed. See Gonda "Pravargya Mantras":259. This implies a less complete correspondence between mantra and activity.

19 The word sūnṛtā is often found in hymns to the Dawn, see Renou *ÉVP* 3:17 and 4:58.

Renou arrives, on the basis of contextual reasons, at the translation "libéralité”

(“generosity”), contrasting with etymologically reconstructed meanings like "virtue, quality of a great-hearted man. "Here we should probably understand sūnṛtā, sc. *vák* ("speech"). For, not only the commentators have: yajñātmikā *satyā vāk* (Bh) and yajfsa-sambandhini mantragatapriyavākyarūpā *devī* (S). But we also have the Kaṭha explanation *vág vaí devi* sūnṛtā (KaṭhĀ 2,6). The unselfish liberality of vāg *devī* is elsewhere often expressed by characterizing her as a milch cow (see Gonda, Pūṣan and *Sarasvatī* (Amsterdam-Oxford-New York, 1985): 44). This by no means contradicts the TĀ expression *yajñó* vai sūnṛtā, because we find the close relation between sacrificial worship (yájfía*)* and speech (vák) more than once expressed in the form of a complete identification (Gonda, *Pūṣan* and Sarasvatī:35-36, §23). Cf. in later Sanskrit sūnṛtā *vāk* as described in a verse of the poet Bhavabhūti. After having stated that *rākṣasī* vāk, bad speech of the arrogant and conceited, is the source of all enmity and the doom of the world, he continues: "The wise call truthful and agreeable speech [sūnṛtā vāk] a cow, the mother of all blessings; a cow that grants desires, removes misery, begets fame and destroys sin" (Bhavabhūti, Uttararāmacarita, Act V, verse 31; tr. M. R. Kane 1924, reprint Delhi-Varanasi-Patna 1982).

20 The hero (vira) is in the context of this rite the Mighty Hero (mahāvirá), i.e. the vessel or the sun. S and Bh take ácchā virám *náryam* parktírādhasam with *devấ yajñám nayantu naḥ* in d. But Geldner, translating the identical RV 1, 40, 3 and following Uv. on VS 33, 89, takes it with b, *prá devy* ètu sūnṛtā.

21 With "For *Makha you*,”he carries away the clay that has been dug out and with *"For the* head of Makha *you"* he throws it down on the black antelope skin *(Śrautakosa* vol.II, Engl.part 1:29ff).

22 Here, the fourth shovelful corresponds to the unbounded (encompassing and going beyond the three worlds: earth, firmament and heaven) and to silence.

23 This is again followed by *"Today may* I *realize* the *head* of the *Sacrifice;* for *Makha you*, for the *head* of Makha you." The mantra, referring to the myth of the earth being taken up from the primeval waters by the boar, is used with the taking of earth, dug up by a boar, one of the prescribed ingredients. Cf. Krick, *Feuergründung*:145-155.

24 The material from the ant-hill is taken with: *dévir vámrir ásya* bhūtásya prathamajā *ṛtāvariḥ, "Divine* ants, first-bom *of* creation, *following* the cosmic *law*" and again

[[P110]]

> "J "Today, may I (The Kaṭhas have *vamriyo* instead of vámrir). In the ant-hill, subterranean materials (the essence of the earth) are built into a stronghold, together with pieces of wood etc. and the saliva of the ants. It stands for nutritious material. Because of its direct connection with subterrestrian regions it is the "ear of the earth.” Cf. Krick, Feuergründung:139-144; for the "ear of the earth" note 368 on p. 368.

"

25 (a) The *sambhará* of the pūtika-plants is accompanied by: *indrasyáujo’si, “*You *are Indra's power*" and "Today, may *I*... (TĀ 4, 2, 4(13)). C omits the first part of the mantra; the deficient number of phrases in the daśati shows that the part, which is present in the other two editions, should be there. The Pūtika plants appear to be grass or herbs that grow in clumps or tussocks that have some firmness. They are different from those plants referred to by the same name in later Sanskrit: Zysk, *Religious* Healing:260; Meulenbeld, Mādhavanidana:537. In the KaṭhĀ 2, 11+ they are a kind of ósadhi, i.e. a herb or herb-like plant. "When Indra slew Vṛtra, his manly energy left him. It entered the herbs (*óṣadhih*). They became the Pūlikas. (The mantra for the Pūtikas is therefore:) *"You* are *Indra's* power." If he collects the Pūtikas as ingredients, he collects Indra's power as ingredient."

(b) The Pūtika is here one of the ingredients of the clay for the Pravargya vessel. But elsewhere it is also considered to be a substitute for the Soma plant itself (see Introduction, note 35). In "The Mahāvīravessel and the plant Pūtika (sic)” (JAOS 95/2 (1975): 222-235), S. Kramrisch sought to defend the hypothesis that the Pūtika was a mushroom, the consumption of which would bring about some psychotropic effects. This Pūtika/Ūtika passage of the TĀ and the passage of the KaṭhĀ translated in part (a) of this note, however, if they are looked at more carefully, clearly indicate that the Pūtika was anything but a mushroom. Bh on TĀ 4, 2, 4 (edition M: 7, 2, 5(14a)) calls the Pūtika “dark Dūrvā-grass" (*śyāmā* dūrvāḥ pūtīkāḥ). Dārila on KauśS 22, 15 calls them tṛṇāni prasiddhāni*,* “the well-known kind of grass or herb." Cf. F. B. J. Kuiper, "Was the Pūtika a mushroom?” in *Amṛtadhārā*:219-227. The most important cornerstone in Kramrisch's argument is the identification of the Sanskrit Pūtika with the Santal Putka, which Kuiper shows to be unjustified on linguistic grounds (op. cit., pp. 222-225). In cases like the identification of the Vedic Pūtika plant or the Soma plant, it seems more advisable to admit how little we know, than to build far-flying theories on the basis of hypotheses that can neither be proved nor disproved. A well-balanced approach is exemplified in R. P. Das' "On the identification of a Vedic plant" in: Studies *on Indian* Medical *History* (ed. by G. J. Meulenbeld and D. Wujastyk, E. Forsten, Groningen 1987): 19-42. In this article the author discusses the case of Pāṭhā, another plant (again most probably not a mushroom) mentioned as substitute for the Soma plant. Useful bibliographical references to the Soma controversy can be found in a recent article by Harry Falk, in which he rejects the Soma-mushroom theory and argues (not entirely convincingly) that the Soma plant was Ephedra (Harry Falk, “Soma I and II," *Bulletin* of *the* School *of* Oriental and African Studies, Univ. of London, Vol. 52, part 1 (1989): 77-90).

[[P111]]

26 *I* # This mantra of the fifth and last requisite is again followed by "*Today*, may Bh has here goat's milk as the fifth ingredient, but at TĀ 4, 2, 4(15) he mentions the mixture of the hair of a she-goat and the hair on the skin of a black antelope. S and most of the Śrauta Sūtras have this mixture of the two kinds of hair added with this mantra. Only BSS prescribes that goat's milk should be made to trickle over the clay with this mantra, while the two kinds of hair are added (in accordance with TĀ) a little later, when the ingredients have been carried to the specially prepared and fenced in mound. See also van Buitenen, *Pravargya*:57, §15 with notes. Baudhāyana, who has given us the oldest Taittirīya Śrauta Sūtra, follows the TĀ again most closely. Also KaṭhĀ and SB refer to goat's milk at the corresponding places. Rau, however, interpreted this passage only in the light of the later Śrauta Sūtras *(Töpferei*:60, note 1 and 2).

27 They take the ingredients to an enclosed plot, north of the fires, in the Pracinavamsa. Rau *(Töpferei*:60) inaccurately in sing.: "Eine Sühnung eben vollzieht er dabei” (“He makes a recompense at it”). Moreover, *ápaciti* had been better derived from a different root ci-: what is meant is that by taking it up by many people, they pay reverence to it. So also S.

28 The Kaṭhas have mádhunā instead of *madhulấ*: “mádhu *tvā* mádhunā karotv*”íty apá* úpa *sṛjati*  
mádhu vấ etán mádhunā *sám* yauti  
*madhavyở bhavati* (KaṭhĀ 2, 14). The form *madhula*, which is less common in later regular Sanskrit and probably original in the mantra, is also in MS 4, 9, 1; 121, 6. CF. RV 1, 191, 10.

29 *armakapāláni*, “potsherds from an *árma*," also in TS 5, 1, 6, 2. KS 19, 5, and KapS 30, 3 have armyaiḥ *kapālaiḥ*, Burrow interpreted *árma* as a term for the ruined cities of the Indus civilization, and thus as a piece of literary evidence in regard to the overthrow of the Indus civilization by the Vedic Aryans (Burrow, "On the significance of the term árma, ármaka in early Sanskrit literature" in the *Journal* of Indian History, 1963:159- 166*)*. But Rau convincingly shows that *pur* and its synonyms in Vedic literature do not refer to anything like the architecturally complex Mohenjo Daro-Harappa cities (Rau, *The* meaning of *púr*, and *Zur vedischen Altertumskunde*:36-38), R. Gopal's “lake" for árma in this compound (“Refutation of T. Burrow's theory on the significance of *árma* and armaka*,*" All*-*India Oriental Conference, Poona 1978) is improbable and unattractive, because with this interpretation the opposition with *grāmya*, “belonging to a (to a greater or lesser extent wandering) community" is lost and the whole passage becomes meaningless.

*árma* therefore means in this context: "deserted village (where a community was temporarily settled)” and *grāmya:* belonging to a (semi-nomadic) community.

30 *śárkarāḥ,* according to Rau pieces of limestone ("Kalksteinbrökchen” Töpferei:60). Krick (*Feuergründung*:162) draws the attention to "das Wesen der Kiesel als 'Festbanner' (das Gute, Eigene herbei; das Böse hinaus)," "the nature of pebbles as 'hold-banisher' (the good, the own nature here, the evil out).”

[[P112]]

### Anuvāka 3

31 *brahma*-varcas*-*á has the suffix -a-of the "neuter determinative compound which is not-collectival, but which must originally have been intended as collectival, and in which the last member is based on a neuter in -as, -us" (tr. from Wackernagel-Debrunner, *Altindische* Grammatik, II (Göttingen 1957), 1:113). Scholars differ considerably in their translation of this frequently occurring word. Keith's "splendour" and Whitney's "vedic splendor" (AV 19, 71) are no doubt too flat. Translations like "the prestige of a priest or of a Brahmin" ("priesterliches Ansehen," Caland, Krick; "Brahmanenansehen,” Witzel) stress the social aspect of this notion too much. The context of our rite asks for a translation that expresses some directly visible aspect of one who has *brahman* or spiritual knowledge (cf. note 89 below), so: "glow or lustre of spiritual knowledge." The lustre of the sun is made concrete, as it were, in the glowing vessel, and should be realized in the participants of the rite as well. That brahmavarcasa shines or glows as it were, becomes clear also from JB 2, 130, where it is said that one whose face is besmeared with the remains of the Soma beverage becomes brahmavarcast. Cf. JUB 3, 13, 1: bhatīva *hi* brahmavarcasam, Oertel: "For lustre in sacred lore shines, as it were"; KS 11, 5: *svetayās* svetávatsāyā *ájyam* bhávati, Suklá vriháya: *evám* iva vaí brahmavarcasám (sc. *évetám* and Śuklám). Compare Weber, *IS* 10 (1868): 105, and the following passage in the well-known story of Satyakāma Jābāla in the Chandogya- Upaniṣad: brahmavid *iva* vai, saumya*, bhāsi,* ko *nu* tvānuṣāseti. “You shine, my friend, like a man who knows *brahman*. Who instructed you?” (ChU 4, 9, 2).

32 *prāṇa* means in the first place "breath," "breathing out," but acquired in plural the meaning of "vital powers" and even "sense functions." Cf. below TĀ 5, 4, 4 and see Bodewitz, *JBtrans):*21, note 2; 47, note 9; and in *ALB* 50 (1986): 326-348.

33 Because the Pravargya vessel is connected with the sun, it will afflict the breath *(prāṇa*) with its *sức*, which means: "the scorching heat of sun or fire," and secondarily: "suffering." During the Agnyādheya ceremony one should not come between the fire and the sun (ĀpŚS 5, 14, 10-11); Cf. Oldenberg, *Weltanschauung*:

120 "offenbar bewegen sich zwischen der Sonne und dem mit ihr identischen Agni Krāfte, die man stören oder durch die man beschādigt werden würde, kāme man ihnen in den Weg." ("apparently, between the sun and Agni, who is identical with the sun, powers are travelling which one would disturb or by which one would be damaged, if one would come in their way.")

34 The yajus-formula is TĀ 4, 2, 5(21); *áditis* te *bílam* gṛhṇātu  
*pānktena chándasā*, "*Let Aditi take your* bowl with the *Pankti*-metre.”

35 The distinction to be established by the mantra is the distinction between vessels for daily use, and the vessels for the Pravargya ceremony,

36 "So big he makes it" has, according to S, to be accompanied by a gesture that indicates one *pradeśa* (the span from the tip of the thumb to that of the forefinger). More than once we come across indications like this, that the Vedic texts originally belonged to * *

[[P113]]

an oral tradition. Cf. Bodewitz, *JBtransĪ*:143, note 9. According to Rau in his translation *(Töpferei*:61), the first gesture indicates a circumference of "eine Spanne” (*pradeśa*), the second "eine Elle" (approximately yard, 0.91m) and the third "ein Klafter" (approximately = fathom, 1.75m). But S takes the second and third expression with "so big he makes it" as expressions to "praise" the same measure of one *pradesa.* This seems more acceptable.

37 The passage in the KaṭhĀ (2, 21+) has: "(The vessel) measures one pradeśa. For, so big is the mouth (or face: múkha). The mouth of the gods is Agni. (The vessel) becomes commensurate with the mouth and gets a bowl from above; this is to satisfy the deities. One is satisfied by progeny and cattle. The Soma beverage falls to the share of him, who knows thus." Here, unlike the parallel TĀ-passage, the text can be understood without accompanying gestures.

38 *parigrivá* indicates that it has a girdle all around the neck for holding it with the tongs (Kashikar, "Apropos\*:4). The vessel as it is used nowadays (see van Buitenen, *Pravargya*, picture opp. to p. 52; Staal, *Agni* 1:231, plate 28D) has no girdle. It is moreover made on a potter's wheel, which would make it unfit for sacrifice according to the ancient Vedic texts.

39 Both the horse and Agni (who the sun = the Pravargya vessel) are frequently said to be born from Prajāpati. Cf. TB 1, 1, 8,2-3: “Prajāpati created Agni. This one, having become a 'tail-horse' (horse with a remarkable tail), went away from him.” (For ásvo *váro* see Krick,*Feuergründung*:411, note 1105.) Krick drew attention to the fact that the well-known etymological connection between the horse (áśva) and the eye of Prajāpati that swelled *(asvayata)* is facilitated by the identifications Horse = Horse of the sun; sun Eye *(Feuergründung*:392).

40 Edition P has diśo'nuvibhāti*.* One expects an accent on the last syllable of *díso* from *ánu*, and this is what the other two editions have.

41 "He puts brilliant energy in it" (also TĀ 5, 2, 13 and 5, 4, 1): asmin (“it”/“him”) refers to the vessel (in TĀ 5, 2, 13 to the materials for the vessel), but, because of the brahmanical identification, at the same time to the sacrificer.

42 When fire is flaring up, it is sacred to Varuṇa; when it grows less, it is sacred to Mitra (Oldenberg, Weltanschauung:112). The verse or verses with which appeasement of the negative aspects of the hot vessels in the fire is to be brought about, are: TĀ 4, 3, 1(3a-c) = TS 4, 1, 6g: *abhīmam* mahiná *dívam* í mitró *babhūva* sapráthāḥ  
utá śrávasā *pṛthivīm*, "Mitra*,* spreading out, *encompassed* this heaven with *his* might, and *the earth with his fame*." Instead of this verse, the next one can be used: TĀ 4, 3, 2(3d-f) = TS 4, 1, *6h* and 3, 4, 11p: mitrasya *carṣanidhṛtaḥ*  
śrávo *devásya* sānasí *  
dyumnám citrá-* śravastamam, *"*The *victorious fame of* god *Mitra*, *the* supporter of *the people*, *is* the most *brilliantly* glorious lustre*."* (reading, with MS and KS, *sānasí* instead of sānasím; see Keith (*TStransl)* on TS 3,4,11p). BSS, SatyŚS, and MŚS prescribe both verses. For the positive and negative aspects of the asuric god Varuṇa, cf. Gonda, Religionen:76.

[[P114]]

KaṭhĀ 3, 169+ comments on another verse that contains "Mitra" and that is recited during the disposal of the Pravargya implements (KaṭhĀ 3,

169 TĀ 4, 11, 6(16)); “If this one in the form of Varuṇa would burn on the offspring, he would not leave anyone whosoever; having become Mitra (‘a friend”) he burns on the offspring.”

43 Rau reads and translates *purás* (Töpferei:59); the three editions (M, C and P) have the unaccented form. P has punas-on p. 444, but on p. 294 twice (correctly) *puras*.

44 S: "Because the pot is extremely terrible (atyugra) on account of its heat, it is fit for the Asuras, but not for the gods. Let him therefore pour (the milk) over it, for the sake of the gods. Therewith, he makes a gentle, cool vessel for the gods." Cf. above, where the vessel in the fire is said to belong to Varuṇa (who is god and Asura). Extreme things belong to the Asuras. For Varuṇa and the Asura's see also Kuiper, "Basic Concept."

45 The milk is poured with the following yajus-formula (see the corresponding Śrauta Sūtra prescriptions for details), of which only the latter part is discussed in the Brāhmaṇa: gāyatreṇa tvā chándasa cchṛṇadmi*,* traíṣṭubhena tvā *chándasā* cchṛṇadmi, jágatena *tvā chándasā cchṛṇadmi;* chrṇáttu *tvā* væk*,* chṛṇáttu *tvórk,* chṛṇáttu tvā *bavíḥ; chṛndhí vácảm, chṛndhy* úrjam, *chṛndhí havíḥ,* *"With* the *gayatri*-metre *I pour over* you, with *the* *tristubh*-*metre I* pour *over you, with the* jagati-*metre I* pour *over you;* let *Speech pour* out on *you*, *let food pour out* on you, *let the* oblation pour out *on* you; pour out *Speech*, pour out *food, pour* out *the* oblation.”

### Anuvāka 4

46 TĀ 4, 4, has a more elaborate praiṣa: “O Brahmán, *we shall* proceed with the Pravargya; o Hot, praise the Gharma; o *Agaidh*, put on *the Rauhiṇa* cakes; *o Pratiprasthātṛ*, *lay* out; *o* Prastot*, sing the* sāmans.

47 Since the Petersburger's Wörterbuch it is usual to translate the word sam-*ardhayati*:, if it occurs with an instrumental, with "supply with" (Keith, e.g. TS 1, 5, 1, 4; 6, 1, 9, 7), "unite" ("vereinigen," Caland, ĀpŚS 11, 20, 1). In many or all of these places a translation that does more justice to the etymology of the word would fit equally well in the context. Being the causative or tenth class conjugation of *√ṛdh-* (“grow, increase, prosper, succeed") with the prefix sam- "completely," translations like those of Witzel (in similar contexts as those of the above mentioned translations) are to be preferred: KaṭhĀ 3, 1514 “Damit lāsst er ihn gedeihen" ("With this he makes him prosper"); 2, 23+ "mit Hilfe der Gestalt (Prajāpatis) lāsst er den (Opferherrn) voll-Erfolg erlangen” (“by means of the form (of Prajāpati) he makes the (sacrificer) obtain complete success"). A translation like "to bring to complete realisation, to bring to fulfilment" fits very well in the context of the head of the Sacrifice that should be realized, or a god who receives an oblation or offering. It would also fit e.g. KaṭhĀ 3, 192+ better than Witzel's "lāsst zusammenkommen mit” (“makes come together with”).

[[P115]]

48 According to Caland on ĀpŚS 15, 6, 4, the materials are sprinkled in such a way that when he sprinkles a second time, he starts again where he began, so not to and fro. With regard to the sacrificer, “in due order" means according to S *pitṛputrādikrameṇa*, which suggests that the several utensils of the Pravargya are sprinkled in order of importance: first the main pot, then the two spare ones and the other utensils.

49 The Hotṛ recites the Brahma-jajñānam hymn: ṚVKhila 3, 22, 1-4; (3 of the verses are AV 4, 1, 1; 2; 4cd, 5ab). See Introduction, section 3.1, p 14; and Gonda, “Pravargya Mantras":238 and 247-248. The wordings of this hymn, the first recitation of the Hotṛ, show that it is intimately connected with the Pravargya ceremony. It was probably already connected with the earliest forms of the Pravargya.

50 *ánavānam,* without taking breath, also TĀ 5, 7, 9. Caland, ĀpŚS 15, 10, 8 translates "ohne auszuatmen" ("without breathing out"). Because during speaking always some breath has to go out, it seems better to take it as "without breathing in” (Śrautakośa 2, Engl. Sect. 1:198) or "without taking breath." The Ṛgvedic ĀśvŚS explains that each verse should be recited without breathing, that the *praṇava* should be uttered at the end and that then there is a pause for breathing (Ā§v§S 4, 6, 1).

51 Usually there are five praná-s. The Śrauta Sūtras prescribe seven or eleven Prāṇāhutis, or prāṇa-oblations with clarified butter (not to be confused with the Prānāhutis of the Prāṇagnihotra, for which see Bodewitz, *JBtransl*:331, note 2). The seven prānas of the head (*śīrṣaṇyà*, unlike múkhya not or rarely with a figurative sense "principal") are enumerated in TĀ 4, 5, 1(3): (1) *prāṇā,* (2) *vyāná,* (3) *apānā,* (4) cákṣus, (5) śrótra, (6) mánas*,* (7) vấk sárasvati*.* Note that *vyāna* and manas are normally not śīrṣaṇya, belonging to the head. They are respectively placed in the middle of the body (or diffused in the body) and in the heart. For the introduction of vital powers and sense-functions in the series of prānas, see Bodewitz, *JBtransl*:220. Four additional prāṇāhutí*-s* can be offered to (8) dákṣa, (9) *krátu*, (10) *ójas,* and (11) *bála.* Cf. note 79*,* 80 and 128.

52 (a) Read with C and P *tejasaivainam* instead of *tejasaivenam* in M.

(b) When the vessel, being held over the Āhavanīya fire, is anointed or filled with ghee (van Buitenen, *Pravargya*:72), the Prastot sings the *śārga* sāman, composed on RV 9, 86, 43, that is, on a verse to Soma Pavamana. In TĀ 5, 11, 4 the Pravargya's name is Soma when it is poured over (sc. with ghee).

53 See above, note 41.

54 This mantra continues with: sócasva devavítamaḥ  
ví dhūmám agne *aruṣām miyedhya  
* srjá prasasta darśatám, *"Be scorchingly* hot as the one *who is* the *best* inviter *of* the gods, o *Agni,* partaker of *the sacrifice*, send forth your *reddish* and beautiful smoke, *you who* are praised."

55 This passage seeks to explain why it is here not the Hotṛ who pronounces the prayers of the sacrificer, but the sacrificer himself (see below). The answer seems to be that in a sacrifice where the prayers precede the main ritual acts (like in the sacrifice under discussion), the sacrificer himself pronounces these prayers.

[[P116]]

56 The formulas are (TĀ 4, 5, 3/4(9)):

*anādhṛsya* purástāt I agnér *ádhipatye | ấyur* me dāḥ *|| putrávati* dakṣiṇatáḥ *i índrasyadhipatye* | *prajam* me *dāḥ* ||  
suṣádā *pascất* | devásya savitúr *ádhipatye* | prāṇam me dah #  
*áśrutir* uttaratáḥ *I mitraváruṇayor ádhipatye I srótram* me *dāḥ ||*  
*vídhṛtir upáriṣṭāt* | *bṛhaspáter* ádhipatye i brahma me dāḥ kṣattrám me dāḥ | *téjo* me *dhā*  
*varco* me *dhāḥ | yáśo* me *dhás tápo* me *dhāḥ i* máno me *dhāḥ*,

> *“(*You, *the eastern* quarter, are) *invincible* in the east, *under* Agni's *overlordship*; grant *me* long *life.*

(You are) rich in sons in the *south*, *under* Indra's *overlordship;* grant me *progeny*.

*(You are*) *good* to *dwell* in in *the west*, *under the overlordship* of *the* god *Savitr*; grant me *prāna*.

*(You are*) hearing in the *north*, under the *overlordship of Mitra-Varuṇa*; grant me the *faculty* of *hearing*.

*(You* are) separated maintenance (of *high and low*) above, under *the overlordship* of *Bṛhaspati*, grant me *spiritual knowledge* (*brahman*)*,* grant *me lordly power*, grant me brilliant energy, grant me lustre, grant me fame, grant *me tapas*, grant me *mind.* \*\* The sacrificer pronounces these respective mantras while he holds the span of his hand over the vessel, first on the east side of the vessel, then on the south, the west, the north and lastly in the middle above the vessel. (Caland on ĀpŚS 15, 7, 6.) Cf. the mythical passage that precedes these mantras in the KaṭhĀ: "The quarters feared the Mahāvīra vessel, which had been put on the fire: 'He will burn us all away with his glow!' They ran towards Prajāpati (for help). Prajāpati said: 'He burns you on every span. I will hide you with the deities.' Therefore, (the sacrificer) metes out the quarters with the span of his hand." (KaṭhĀ 2, 79+)

57 The following passage shows that according to the author of this text, the sacrificer turns in all directions, while pronouncing the above mentioned formules (note 56). This seems to be incompatible with the prescription in some Śrauta Sūtras (BhŚS, ĀpŚS and HirŚS), that the fingers should always be pointing towards the east.

58 The Maruts are usually atmospheric, very tempestuous powers of storm (Gonda, *Religionen* 1:61), But the Ṛgvedic adjectives used to describe their outlook are words like: viśvábhānu*,* svábhānu*,* hiraṇyáya*, súryatvac,* expressing a sunlike character. In RV 5, 55, 3 they are suryasyeva *raśmáyaḥ*. The sunlike outlook of the Maruts should originally perhaps not be connected with rays of the sun, but rather with their celestial, divine character and possibly with the lightning of their storms.

59 This passage of Agni and the Vikarkaṭa wood is approximately TB 1, 1, 3, 12.

60 The additional thirteenth month is sometimes identified with the year itself. An additional element often comprises and synthesizes the other elements, e.g. when a fourth item is added to a three-fold totality. The year is often identified with the sun and JB 1, 50 connects the thirteenth month directly with the sun: eṣa trayodaśo *ya eṣa* tapati. (“The

[[P117]]

thirteenth month is he who burns yonder (the sun).") See Gonda, *Prajāpati* and the Year:23; Bodewitz, "The Waters in Vedic Cosmic Classifications," Indologica *Taurinensia* 10 (1982), especially p. 47; Bodewitz, JBtransl:59-60, note 4.

61 This mantra should accompany the placing of the thirteenth enclosing stick. The corresponding Kaṭha-fragment has: "With: '*You* are the concealment *of* the firmament', he puts the fuel-stick on top. With wood he conceals it from above." The Adhvaryu and the Pratiprasthātṛ place the twelve sticks around the pot in three layers, with the formulas "You are measure,” etc. (van Buitenen, *Pravargya*:§17). They are placed at four sides; for Caland's remark (on ĀpŚS 15, 8, 3) that the cast remains free, I cannot find any support. Now the thirteenth stick is put down, according to all Sūtras of the Taittirīyas, to the south. Most Sūtras have the stick laid down, but Baudhāyana prescribes it should be fixed up, with its tip pointing upward (*ūrdhvāgram* niminoti*)*. The Sūtra of the Maitrāya-niyas, Manava Śrauta Sūtra, differs from all Taittirīya Sūtras in that it prescribes the placing of the thirteenth stick (together with the gold plate) on top of the Pravargya vessel as a covering. In this way the agreement between action and mantra seems more complete than in the other Sūtras: the *antardhí* is here a real *antar*-*dhi* (from *antar-dhā*, to conceal), and not, like the other twelve sticks, a *pari-dhí.* The KaṭhĀ also speaks of putting the thirteenth stick on top, which according to the context must mean: on top of the vessel. The problem with this interpretation is, however, that the placing of the antardhí is *followed* by the placing of the gold plate on top. If the fuel stick should have been placed there before, ashes would fall into the hot milk vessel during or after burning. Possibly, we are to assume that in the KaṭhĀ and Mānava version of this element of the rite, the thirteenth stick is placed on or near the top but not over the opening. The TĀ, however, gives no indication where the stick should be placed and most Taittirīya Sūtras treat the thirteenth stick as an additional enclosing stick, without giving a special meaning to the word *antardhí* in the mantra. The discrepancy was felt by S who explained inconvincingly - that the stick was to be laid down "with the concealment of the upper region in mind" *(ūrdhvalokadigvyavadhānabuddhyā*).

62 This mantra (TĀ 4, 5, 7(17)) is approximately RV 2, 33, 10. There: abhva*,* instead of here: ábbhuva, *viśvárūpa* is not "many-coloured” but “brilliant as the sun, fire or gold” (Bodewitz, WZKS 1985:15-19). This Rudra mantra, used as a verse of praise of the Pravargya vessel, is better at its place in the "Rudra-oriented" Kaṭha version than here and in the MS (the mantra is not in the VS).

63 For Ugradeva Rājani see Caland, *JB in Auswahl:*300,

64 Compare above TĀ 5, 3, 1, where the same ailment was predicted for one who comes between the Pravargya vessel, while it is being prepared, and the sun. "Having a bad skin": according to the Indian commentators the skin-disease is *kustha*. This name refers to a class of diseases which comprises leprosy. Cf. Zysk, *Religious Healing*:20. A disease called *kuṣtha* is described by Suśruta at Nidāna Sthāna 5, 23, 7.

65 See Staal, *Agni* 1:370-371, plate 57.

[[P118]]

### Anuvāka 5

66 TS 5, 5, 9, 4 also connects "Dyutāna Māruta, together with the Maruts" with the north. Dyutāna, son of the Maruts, is not a very frequently occurring character. TS 1, 3, 1, 2 has a mantra for him. His name is also found in KS 35, 16; VS 5, 17; ŚB 3, 6, 1, 16 and TS 6, 2, 10, 4.

67 *ási*, the finite verb in the first of two antithetical clauses, is accented (MacDonell, *Vedic Grammar* for Students:468). In the immediately following pair of antithetical clauses, one expects the accented *ásí* too, but the unaccented form is actually found.

### Anuvāka 6

68 The Upasads are called the neck (*grívás*, feminine plural, like upasádas) in AiB 1, 25 too, but there the Guest Reception (*ātithyeṣṭhi)* is the head of the Sacrifice.

69 KaṭhĀ 3, 207+ is somewhat more elaborate: "The Jyotiṣtoma (the Soma sacrifice, of which the Agniṣṭoma is the basic form) was in that time the sacrifice. Just like here (in the sacrifice) from fire, when it is born, the other fires are taken, like that the other sacrifices are born from this (Jyotistoma). Therefore, he should perform the Pravargya at the Jyotiṣtoma, for elevation (of the Pravargya).”

70 This and the following sections of this anuvāka would have fitted well in Gonda's enumeration of Vedic texts prescribing "ritual acts performed to derive some advantage from looking on a mighty being or event, to participate in its nature and essence, to be purified or raised to a higher level of existence by being vis-à-vis with such a man or deity or by witnessing such an event” (Gonda, *Eye and* Gaze:55; at other places the author frequently refers to the numerous other instances in this rite where “looking" has some dangerous or beneficial effects). Thus, the priest and the sacrificer get a share in the sunlike power and lustre of the heated vessel. Note that the accompanying mantras aim at establishing a connection between the Pravargya vessel and the sun.

Cf. finally KaṭhĀ on the importance of gazing only with the Avakāśa mantras (see note 79 below): "The gods and the Asuras [did in the Pravargya ceremony] the same. [What the gods did, that the Asuras did.] The gods saw these [mantras for] gazing. With these [mantras they gazed at the Pravargya vessel on the fire. The gods] prospered, the Asuras perished. If one [gazes] at the Pravargya vessel with these [Avakāśa mantras], one prospers and becomes fit [for the sweet beverage]” (KaṭhĀ 3, 226+, text completed by Witzel).

71 While this Āraṇyaka interprets the first words of this well-known verse (TĀ 4, 7, 1(1) = RV 1, 164, 31 = RV 10, 177, 3) in two ways, these two interpretations are not worked out in relation to the rest of the verse. The Kaṭha-fragment on this mantra clearly

[[P119]]

takes only the interpretation of *gopám* as the sun into consideration: “*Wandering* hither *and thither* on his *pathways*.' Therefore he follows all pathways, the southern and northern ones. *'Enveloping* himself in those *(*quarters*) that* converge, in those that *spread* out.' Four indeed are his converging (or; main?) quarters. Four his diverging (or: secondary?) [quarters]. Enveloping himself in these, he covers these worlds with brilliant energy" (KaṭhĀ 2, 102-103+).

72 Edition C reads *gataḥ* in Pravargya mantra TĀ 4, 7, 1(3), with an incorrect visarga. Van Buitenen seems to follow this reading *(Pravargya*:93). The pratīka here and in the KaṭhĀ, as well as the accentuation, show that we have to read (a-)*gata*, either root-aorist or injunctive If we assume an augmented form, the past-tense meaning is incidental or absent, like e.g. in *havír* evákaḥ in anuvāka 3, daśati 9 and *arocista* in the next mantra. For the difference between the injunctive and the corresponding augmented forms, and their relative interchangebility, see K. Hoffmann, *Der* Injunktiv im *Veda,* Heidelberg 1967.

73 C: *tapoja* (without visarga).

74 Tapas is "the generation of creative internal glow, belonging to the brahman methods of rising above normal human conditions and possibilities, of obtaining revelations of secret wisdom and contact with the divine powers" (Gonda, Change *and* Continuity:294). Tapas also means heat (cf. the mantras discussed above in this anuvāka), The root *tap-* is connected with the subjective but concrete physical feeling of heat, contrasting with *dah-* (“burn") that always has objective, visible effects (Blair, *Heat* in the *Rig Veda and* *Atharva Veda*, (New Haven 1969): 89). For the connection between tapas and the gaining of insight and wisdom see Hauwer, *Yogapraxís*:67; Gonda, *Change* and *Continuity*:342; and W. O. Kaelber, “Tapas, Birth and Spiritual Rebirth in the Veda," *History* of *Religions* 15 (1975):343-386.

75 S interprets the "speech, born of tapas" as "speech fit for the sacrifice and in the form of mantras." The tapas may refer to the Avāntaradīkṣā, the period of “tapas” in which the mantras are studied and certain vows are undertaken.

76 "Germ of *the* gods”: Agni/Pravargya is here called the *garbha* (germ or embryo) of the gods. S: *devair* apekṣitasya *haviṣo'tra garbharūpēṇāvasthānāt.* “because the offering, to which the gods look forward, is here in the form of a garbha." The *garbha* is the subject to be fostered, growing fast or about to undergo a total transformation. This term should be understood in the light of the symbolism of the initiated sacrificer (*dikṣitā*) as gárbha. Cf. Krick, Feuergründung:96. The sacrificer who is consecrated for the sacrifice *(dikṣita*), will come out reborn from the final bath. The garbha of the armies, who should come out of the battle reborn (as the victor), is the King (Krick, *Feuergründung*:113, note 291). ŚB explains differently: he is the *garbha* of the gods because he holds *(grabh-*) everything.

77 *matí,* here meaning thoughts that acquired the shape of hymns and formulas, *matí, dhi*, *maniṣā* are words belonging to the same semantic field. All these words can have a concrete and a more abstract sense (Cf. Gonda, *Vision*:7-13). Here the concrete

[[P120]]

to *74* manifestations of the "inspired thought" are meant. Below, *máti* (note the accent) is used in its more abstract sense: “(inspired) thought of the poets."

78 C and P have the svarita accent on the first syllable, which is what one expects.

79 The nine Avakāśa mantras (TĀ 4, 7, 1(1)ff) are according to S: 1. "I saw the *herdsman*... 2-7: the mantras relating to the six seasons; 8 and 9: the non-metrical rest of TĀ 4, 7, up to *"May I* as *one who is* associated with *Tvastr.* "The words ava-kāṣá and ā-kāsá may mean "the place where (the sense-function or vital power) manifests itself." See Bodewitz, *JBtransl*:56, note 5: “Here the *praṇaḥ* are not lifebreaths or vital powers, but the organs of sense, or rather the orifices through which the gods (= vital powers, sense-functions) enter.... The *ākāśāḥ* are in fact every kind of opening.” But the root *kās-* has not only the meaning of "to be visible," but also of "to see or survey,” e.g. in the intensive *cākasīti.* Thus, *avakāśa* is the technical name of "certain mantras during the recitation of which the eyes must be fixed on a particular object" (Gonda, Eye and *Gaze*:22; and see KaṭhĀ 3, 226+ in note 70). These Avakāśa mantras are here connected with the nine apertures of the human body and the pranas that manifest themselves there. The Avakāśa mantras and the praṇas are also connected in SB 14, 1, 4, 1 and 2, 2, 51.

80 Cf the seven prāṇas in anuvāka 4 and note 51 Here the two organs of secretion are probably added to this series of seven pranas. Cf. the Bhagavad Gitā, 5, 13, where *the* body is called a nine-gated stronghold.

81 *sápa* is in all texts interpreted as *sepha*, "male organ," although an etymological connection between the two does not exist (Mayrhofer, Concise *Etymological Sanskrit Dictionary*, vol. 3 (Heidelberg 1976), s.v.). It should be connected with the root sap-, “to adore, honour (gods); to love (also sexually).” Cf. Caland on ĀpŚS 10, 23, 8.

### Anuvāka 7

82 The verb, itself unaccented, is compounded with two verbal prepositions, like in the TS. See Weber, *IS* 13(1873): 62-63.

83 I take (you)" belongs to the former mantra *"You*, at *the impulse of the god Savitṛ*, etc." S: "because its sense is clear, the citation is its explanation."

84 The Adhvaryu has taken the thether for the cow with the former mantra and speaks now this mantra over it.

85 The Adhvaryu pronounces the divine names softly. Then he goes out through the southern door and pronounces the cow's own name in a loud voice. Thus, BSS, BhSS and ĀpŚS have the Adhvaryu first go out through the eastern door; then, they have him call the cow softly by her divine names. Then he is to return, go out through the southern door and call the cow in a loud voice by her own name. HirŚS, KŚS and MŚS are slightly different (Śrautakosa, Engl. part 1:192-207). “Southern gate" is to be read instead of "eastern gate" in the last section on p. 95 of van Buitenen's description.

[[P121]]

86 For a different application of this verse see Bṛhad-Āraṇyaka-Upaniṣad (BĀU) 6, 4, 27: "Then (the father) hands the (new born babe) to his mother and gives him her breast, saying 'This breast of thine, *ever flowing* etc.""; cf. also Pāraskara Gṛhya Sūtra 1, 16, 21.

87 S explains: "For in everyday life (loke) the mother of a child asks the master of the house: ‘give milk for the child'; that is how it should be understood.”

88 *upasidatu*: the verb is used in the sense of "to approach (respectfully) in order to milk," cf. Bodewitz. Agnihotra, Leiden 1978:56 note 3: "In connection with cattle it seems to denote careful attention and waiting upon them (cf. AV 3, 14, 6) perhaps especially by milking them.

"

89 Bṛhaspati is connected with the brahman-priest in the ritual and with *brahman* (neuter), "spiritual knowledge," or, more specific, "le pouvoir formulateur” (Renou, “Les Pouvoirs de la Parole dans le Ṛgveda” in *ÉVP* 1 (1955): 12), so "the power to formulate," i.e. the power to express spiritual knowledge in bráhmans: hymns, formulas or enigmatic cosmic equations.

90 Van Buitenen translates vigy-ag —ft with "surrounded by (blood)," possibly connecting or confusing it with *viṣv-ag*—*vr-ta* that occurs in this meaning in later Sanskrit. But *vṛt* cannot be pressed into the meaning "surround." The context suggests a meaning like Caland's "(von dem Blute) euch abwendend" ("turning away (from the blood)"). This translation is not without problems either, because *viṣu* does not usually mean "away from." The word *visv*-anc however appears to have in some cases the meaning "separated or different from," if construed with instrumental or ablative (Petersburger Wörterbuch vol. 6:665, referring among other places to TS 1, 5, 9, 7 and 2, 3, 2, 6). In our case we have the instrumental lõhitopa. Moreover, there is a word viṣū-vṛt. This probably means “indifferent to, not partaking of" in RV 10, 43, 3, where it is connected with two ablatives (thus PW, 6:1258; different: Grassman and Geldner). Although the meaning cannot be ascertained beyond any doubt in this way, Caland's translation is still the most acceptable one.

91 The Adhvaryu and his assistant have now finished milking and given the pails with the milk to the Agnīdhra. They have gone to the Vedi, where they take the tongs and the supporting vessel (van Buitenen, *Pravargya:* 100).

92 The *upayama* or *upayamana* is according to S a wooden vessel (kāṣṭhāviśeṣa, pātraviśeṣa, *kāṣṭhāpātra*: S on TĀ 4, 8, 4(13); (20); 11, 3(6)), but van Buitenen's picture (Pravargya, plate 3-3, facing p. 52) shows a ladle. The *Śrautakośa* has “supporting ladle.”

93 This verse is pronounced by the Adhvaryu when looking upon the milk that is now being brought in by the Agnīdhra (who also comes to the Vedi; van Buitenen, *Pravargya:*100).

94 The verse accompanies the pouring of the cow's milk into the hot vessel (van Buitenen, *Pravargya*:101-102).

95 juhomi: in this anuvāka this verb signifies the pouring into the hot vessel of the cow's milk and the goat's milk. The actual offering is the subject of the next anuvāka.

[[P122]]

96 *vani* is an action noun from Vvan-. This verb is often used in connection with a god who wins something for his worshipper, e.g. RV 1, 36, 17bc; 1, 162, 22d; 7, 15, 4c. What is said by Grassmann on the root *san-* (Wörterbuch zum Ṛgveda, p. 1465) applies here too: the notion splits in two: "to obtain" and "to give," and often it is difficult to decide between the two, because according to the Vedic point of view "obtaining" on the part of the gods results in "giving." Here, the sun's ray gains rain in order to give it. It contains a reference to the well-known idea that water is sucked up by a sun-ray, so that it can pour it down in the form of rain (See TĀ 5, 10, 6 below, and e.g. Lüders, *Varuṇa* 1:308ff: “Die Sonne als Spenderin des Regens").

97 With this and/or the following phrase, the Adhvaryu should gaze at the glow from the milk in the hot vessel. There are several differences in the details between the Śrauta Sūtras; see van Buitenen, *Pravargya*:102; *Śrautakosa*,

2 Engl. 1:192 and 198.

98 "*With* heaven *and* earth I encompass you"; the Pratiprasthātṛ takes hold of the Pravargya vessel by means of the tongs. The vessel now contains the milk of the cow and the goat (van Buitenen, *Pravargya*:103).

99 He supports it by means of the supporting vessel.

100 The space between the western or Gārhapatya fire (representing this world or the earth) and the eastern or Āhavanīya fire (representing yonder world or heaven) is connected with the firmament and the Wind. Compare TĀ 5, 11, 4, where the Pravargya is sacred to Vāyu, the Wind, when being brought (sc. to the Āhavanīya).

101 The Pravargya, filled with the milk-mixture, is lifted up and taken to the Āhavanīya fire for the offering.

102 ánavanam: see above, note 50.

103 The five names are taken from the following seven formulas (the last two are optional according to S): samudraya tvā vátāya sváha *salilaya* tvá *vátāya sváhā*  
anādhṛṣyẩya tvā *vātāya* sváhā  
apratidhṛṣyấya *tvā* vātāya sváhā i *avasyáve* tvā *vấtāya* sváhā *  
* dúvasvate *tvā vātāya* sváhā 1 símidvate *tvā* vātāya svấhā, (1) *“You* to the ocean (of *air or atmospheric* waters), *to the Wind,* svāhā*!*" (2) *“You* to what *is* waving *(salila),* to the *Wind*, *svāhā!”* (3) *“*You *to* the unassailable, *to* the *Wind*, *svāhā*!" (4) *"You to* the irresistible, to *the Wind*, *svāhā!”* (5) “You *to him* who *desires* to *help*, to the *Wind, svāhā!”* (6) “You *to the* favourable, *to* the *Wind*, svāhā!*”* (7) *“*You to *śimidvat*, to *the Wind, svaha!"*

(For (2), *salilá,* see Gonda, *Sel*. *Studies* 3:432, and 419 note 41; the name *símidvat* (*7*) is unclear; it also occurs at MS 4, 9, 8.)

104 Cf. KaṭhĀ 2, 129. The mantras of the Vajasaneyins are slightly different (VS 38, 8, 9; SB 14, 2, 2, 6ff).

105 havis*-*prakṣepābhāve'pi svāhā*-sabda-prayoga*-mātreṇa *juhotīti* (S): “although there is no pouring of the oblation from one vessel to the other, he still pours in (juhotī*)* merely by using the word 'svāhā!”” to *80*

[[P123]]

106 Of the six mantras referred to here (TĀ 4, 9, 1/2(1)), only five are used. Together with the first five names of the Wind this makes ten. The SB prescribes 12 names of the Wind.

107 The southern Rauhina-cake is offered by the Pratiprasthātṛ, before the milk-offering, with "Let day etc." in the morning and with "Let night etc." in the evening. These two mantras also belong to the offering of the northern Rauhina-cake (e.g. ĀpŚS 15, 11, 5 and 8). In TĀ 4 these mantras are not found after 4, 9, 1/2(1) (which would be expected on the basis of the sequence in this chapter), but at the point where the northern Rauhina-cake is offered, i.e. after the milk-offering, and mostly also after the offering of the six woodsplinters (TĀ 4, 10, 4(7); ĀpŚS and BhŚS: before the offering of the six woodsplinters).

108 In Caland's translation of this verse (TĀ 4, 10, 4(7), in ĀpŚS 15, 10, 10), followed by van Buitenen *(Pravargya*:107), *jyótis-* is in apposition with the subject, while the verb *juṣ-* is construed with an instrumental instead of the usual accusative. It is better to take *jyótis-* as the object. In that way, the appositional relation of *jyótis-* with *rấtri-* in the latter part, being hardly acceptable, is avoided, and √juṣ-is regularly construed with the accusative.

109 The ketu-, "banner, torch, brightness," is the Rauhiṇa-cake that is being offered in the Āhavanīya fire (Caland, ĀpŚS 15, 9, 2 and 10, 10). Being saturated with ghee, it flares up. For *jótiṣām* in M read *jyótiṣām*.

### Anuvāka 8

110 While pronouncing this verse-quarter, the Adhvaryu looks at the Brahmán priest. "And he protects him from failure in sacrifice" refers to the expiatory function of this priest who is the generalist, not specialized in one of the three Vedas. The knowledge of the expiations for different mistakes and calamities during the sacrifice arises from his more general knowledge of the cosmic background of the rite. This general, universal knowledge expresses itself both in his silence and in his power to put this knowledge into formulas (bráhmans, see note 89). He is connected with the south, probably because he is the one who protects against dangerous aspects of this quarter that belongs to Death (cf. below: "what (overflows) in the south, that is for the fathers") and the Asuras. According to Bodewitz, the south should be considered to represent originally that which encompasses and transcends the differentiated creation (ie. the undifferentiated totality), rather than that which is in opposition to this creation (Bodewitz, "The Fourth Priest (Brahmán) in Vedic Ritual," *Studies* in *the History* of Religions *(Supplements* to *NUMEN),* 45 (1983): 43-45) Cf. Krick, *Feuergründung*:245, note 599, who mentions in the context of the southern fire *(dakṣināgni*) “die - ursprūnglich positive - Asura-Natur dieses Feuers . . ., das als Manenfeuer den Gegenpol zum Āhavaniya der Götter bildet”

[[P124]]

+ ("the - originally positive - asuric nature of this fire ... that formed, as the fire of the forefathers, the counterpole to the Āhavanīya of the gods").

111 The Adhvaryu looks at the Hotṛ.

112 *hārdi-vānám* means "having the heart," "dear to the heart." The Vājasaneyins have *hārdvānám*, a term which the author of the SB seems to consider as obscure: according to him it stands, together with the next word, for "not distinctly expressed,” ániruktam (SB 14, 2, 2, 21).

113 *ūtí-* from Vav-. Note that forms of this root have often *dhi-* or cognate terms as their object (Gonda, *Vision*:127ff). If we take "to favour" as the central meaning, this "favouring" may consist either of giving help or promotion, or of accepting an offer or song or *dhi-* favourably. This last mentioned meaning I take to be applicable in ūtí-in our mantra also. “To accept favourably" *is* one of the meanings of *Vav-* according to Monier- Williams' Dictionary, p. 96. So it means "(your, the Aśvins') favour to accept and enjoy the oblation." Van Buitenen's "help" *(Pravargya*:109: “Drink, o Aśvins, . . . with your help") does not seem to make much sense (but compare RV 1, 46, 15, recited by the Hotṛ just before).

114 áhar-divabhir. The texts have an anomalous double accentuation of this word (also VS 38, 12b; but ŚB 14, 2, 2, 21: *ahardivábhir*, cf. MS 4, 9, 134).

115 For a picture of the offerings from the Pravargya vessel, held with the two tongs in the Āhavanīya fire, see Staal, Agni 1:372-373. The sequence of the formulas and offerings in this section, exactly correspond to Baudhāyana's description. Other Śrauta Sūtras have "*O Aśvins*, drink..." *after* the Adhvaryu's request to the Hoty to recite the yājyā (for the yājyā see ĀpŚS 4, 7, 4 and RV 1, 46, 15; AV 7, 73, 4 and 5).

116 *“Svāhā*, *ye two Indras, vaṭ!”* (van Buitenen, *Pravargya*:109), though grammatically correct, makes no sense. The form *īndrā* probably stands for *indraya* (cf. Bh ad loc. and Caland on ApŚS 15, 10, 11, note 9).

117 *svagakṛtyai* literally means "for the exclamation 'svagā!"" But the exclamation "svagā!" is not uttered at these oblations. This exclamation, far less frequently used than e.g. “svāhā!" and "vaṣaṇ/vaṭ!” probably originally meant "good speed" or "safe journey.” Cf. e.g. TB 3, 3, 8, 11: "Svagā to the divine priests!' he says. Therewith he renders the sacrifice svagā (having a good speed or safe journey towards the gods).” See also Gonda, Pūṣan and Sarasvatī (Amsterdam-Oxford-New York, 1984):130 (§110 and for references note 58). Here, *svagákṛtyai* probably means: “for the wish for a good journey, normally expressed by the exclamation 'svaga!" After one offering to Indra, that was meant "for keeping back the Rakṣases," the second offering can now have "good speed" or a "safe journey" (towards the gods).

118 After the oblation to the Aśvins, the vessel is again filled, either with milk (according to BSS and MŚS), or with cooked curds that have been prepared before (according to ĀpŚS and others). See Caland note 4 *on* ĀpŚS 15, 10, 12. to *84*

[[P125]]

119 TĀ 4, 10, 1(lab). The formula continues (TĀ 4, 10, 1(1c-j): bráhmaṇe pipihi | *kṣattraya* pipihi | adbhyáḥ pipihi | óṣadhibhyaḥ pipihi | *vánaspátibhyaḥ* pipihi | *dyavāpṛthivibhyām pipihi | subhūtāya* pipihi *|* brahmavarcasaya pipihi, “Swell for the *Brahmins*, *swell for the* Kṣatriyas*, swell for the waters, swell* for *the lords* of *the* forest *(*the *trees*), *swell* for heaven *and earth*, swell *for well-*being, *swell* for the lustre of *spiritual knowledge*.” And finally (TĀ 4, 10, 2(1kl)): “*swell for* the *sacrificer*; *swell* for me, for pre-*eminence*” (see text).  
120 Between this part and the following "Lest *the wind*...," the full mantra (TĀ 4, 10, 2(3)) has moreover: *kṣattrāṇi dhāraya |* viśam dhāraya, “*support* the *Kṣatriyas*, support *the Vaisyas*." I prefer to divide: dhárma-asi *sudhárma*-ámeny asmé | brahmāṇi dhāraya *kṣattraṇi* dhāraya visam dharaya. S and van Buitenen, however, read: *dhármā*-asi sudhármấ me *ny* asmé | *bráhmāni* dharaya, etc. The double accent of sudhármá is suspicious; á as preposition or from a compound verb \f-ni-dhr* is unlikely. Bh reads *dharma* and *sudhármā*, and has to explain *ámeny* as ameni (from *ámení* + *in).* This seems rather far-fetched. Moreover, VS 38,14fg has: (f) *dhármāsi sudhárma 1* (g) *ámeny* asmé nṛmṇani dhāraya brahma *dhāraya kṣattrám dhāraya* vísam *dhāraya*.

Another difficulty in the TĀ mantra is *bráhmāṇi*. The parallelism with *kṣattrấni* and *vísam* in the same mantra suggest the interpretation "class of Brahmins," but this is usually expressed by the neuter singular (as in VS 38, 14fg). S explains bráhmāṇi, and kṣattráni as the works to be done by the class of Brahmins and the class of the Kṣatriyas respectively. The KaṭhĀ has: dhármasi sudhármemány asmai bráhmāṇi dhāraya. This looks like a linguistic simplification. The difficulties of *ameni* and *brahmāṇi* are avoided. *brahmāṇi* may refer in this mantra simply to the brahman-formulas, to be supported for the sake of the sacrificer.

121 Below, anuvāka 11 daśati 4, the Pravargya ceremony is sacred to Pūṣan in the stage where the milk reaches the rim of the vessel, but sacred to Mitra when a film has been formed on the boiled milk (*sáro*-*gṛhitaḥ,* also MS 1,8,10). sáras, the cream or film on boiled milk may express Pūṣan's connection with thriving and prosperity.

122 pratírá, "promoting, promoter." The Āraṇyaka takes the word in this abstract sense. Caland (ĀpŚS 15, 11, 6) thinks that it refers to the "sound-holes." He adds in a note: “unsicher!" ("uncertain!”) He probably assumed that it is a short form of the infrequent *pratirava*, that may stand for *uparava* ("sound-hole").

123 For the accentuation of *nir-*áva-*dayati,* see note 82. The verb is often used to denote a sacrifice to Rudra. To eliminate the inauspicious and dangerous aspects of Rudra, this god must be satisfied and appeased. Cf. Arbman, *Rudra.* Untersuchungen zum *altindisch-* en *Glauben und Kultus* (Uppsala, 1922): 55.

124 Because the Adhvaryu has touched the woodsplinter for Rudra, he has lost his sacrificial purity. He regains it by touching water.

125 Cf. Gonda, *Eye and* Gaze:28.

126 C: *áhutir* instead of áhuti.

[[P126]]

127 This passage is the same as TB 1, 1, 6, 9. The meaning in the context of the Pravargya is that the first mantra of the Agnihotra, intended for its evening performance, would be applied at the first performance of the Pravargya, which takes place in the morning. If at the first occasion the formula "Bhūḥ svāhā!” is used instead, the two mantras (“Agni is light, light is Agni, svāhā!" and "The sun is light, light is the sun, svāhā!") will be regularly used in the next evening and morning performances of the Agnihotra. Cf. Bodewitz, Agnihotra, Leiden 1976:172.

128 Prana is the most important one in the five-fold series of vital powers. This series includes at least some powers that also belong to the series of five senses (*indriyani*). See Bodewitz in *ALB* 50 (1986):343. This equation of prāṇa with the fire in which the oblation is offered, seems to foreshadow the identifications underlying the Prāṇāgni-hotra, in which the daily oblations in the fire are replaced by daily offerings of food in the prāṇas. This amounts to the ceremonial consummation of food by the sacrificer. See Bodewitz, "Agnihotra and Prāṇāgnihotra" in JBtransh213-343; and cf. note 51 above.

129 See Staal, *Agni* 1:376-377, plate 60A. Either they all drink the milk (or cat the curds, see note 118 above), or only the sacrificer drinks (or eats) while the priests just smell at it (van Buitenen, Pravargya:117).

130 (a) This passage (the first part of TĀ 5, 8, 13) relates to the period of study before the actual performance of the Pravargya ceremony. By following the observances the pupil "sharpens his brilliant energy" or, more generally, he increases his sacrificial purity and mental power. The observances mentioned here are less severe than those that are found in the Avāmaradikṣā, prescriptions of the Pravargya portions of the Śrauta Sūtras. While in the latter the pupil should not even speak to a woman or a Śūdra, we have in our passage the prohibition to approach a Śūdra woman (*rāmā,* but Keith: "woman of pleasure". TStransi: 462, n. 1) sexually. Whether *rāmā* should be interpreted as *ramaniyaḥ* putraḥ (S; cf. Weber, IS 10 (1868): 74, n. 1), or, more likely, as “Śūdra" (Bh) or "servant," the subject of these observances must be someone in the householder stage of life (*grhastha*). Therefore, it was certainly not the Veda student who followed these observances, but rather the sacrificer. Now, the immediately following mythical passage on Vibhrāj Saurya connects these observances with the Pravargya mantras "to be recited by day” (*divākirtyà*). If they are connected with recitation, they must be connected with a period of study before the actual performance. Perhaps BSS 9,1 also refers to such a period of study by the sacrificer before the Pravargya performance (note 11 above). It is not very likely that a year of study had to precede each performance or continuous series of performances of the Pravargya. It was possibly permitted to represent the year by a few days, as seems to be suggested by SB 14, 1, 1, 28. In our passage the term Avāntarādīkṣā is not used, but if it was considered to be a special form of the Avāntaradīkṣāp, adapted for the sacrificer, the difference of this Avāntaradīkṣā with the initiation rites of the Soma sacrifice, the Dīkṣā and Avāntaradīkṣā,, would have become

[[P127]]

very small. For the way in which this passage of the Āraṇyaka is used and reinterpreted in the Śrauta Sūtras see Introduction section 3.3, p. 24.

(b) Although not all rules in this passage are completely clear as far as their significance is concerned, it seems that in general their observance was believed to preserve and increase the potency of the subject. For the purpose of abstinence from sexual intercourse before the performance of a sacrifice see Gonda's article "Ascetics and Courtesans" (*ALB* 25 (1961): 78-102 = *Select*. 4:223-247; especially pp. 93-95 (238- 240)); "In a considerable number of cases we read also in the Vedic texts that the intending sacrificer must not only fast, sleep on the ground etc., but also abstain from sexual intercourse. It is undesirable that the consecrated man should lose any potency” (95). A person who studied the sacred knowledge had to apply his whole person and all his energy. See also Gonda, Change and *Continuity*, Chapt. 9 on Brahmacarya and Chapt. 10 on Dīkṣā; and W. Kaelber, "Tapas, Birth and Spiritual Rebirth in the Veda," *History of Religions* 15 (1975): 343-386. For the prohibition to drink from a clay or earthenware vessel, sec note 164.

### Anuvāka 9

131 KaṭhĀ 3,174-176 has the metres in reverse order; it connects jagati with heaven, tristubh with the firmament and gāyatrī with the earth.

132 The need for gestures to make the information complete again shows that these texts were meant for "live" recitation.

133 With these verses they all leave the Prācīnavaṃsa, taking the Pravargya implements with them. *They* will bring the implements to the place of disposal. TĀ 4, 11, 2(3) has only the two *pratikas*. The complete verses are in TS 3, 3, 11, land *m*: ánu no '*dyẫnumatir yajñíyām* devéṣu *manyatām* ! *agnisca* havyaváhano *bhávatām dāśúṣe máyaḥ*, "May Anumati *today* for *us* favour *our* sacrifice among the *gods*. *May* she *and* Agni, the bearer *of the oblation*, *be* a joy to the giver*"* and *ány fd* anumate tvám mányāsai *sám* ca nah kṛdhi  
*krátve dákṣāya* no *hinu*  
prá na *ấyūmṣi* tāriṣaḥ, *“You,* o Anumiati, should favour us *and give us well-*being. *Impel* us for intelligence *and skill, lengthen* our *life*-time.'

134 (a) I follow Calland and van Buitenen in translating "for protection," although one would expect in that case the dative *paraspẩyai* in all three mantras. But only TĀ 4, 11, 3(6) has the dative, the other two, TĀ 4, 11, 2/3(4) and TĀ 4, 11, 3(5) have the gen./abl. *paraspāyāḥ*. KaṭhĀ has in this series of three mantras *paraspắm* instead of the genetive and dative, which may be considered a simplification.

(b) The first mantra is recited by the Adhvaryu after the first chanting of the *tyagnāyiḥ* sāman, the second mantra after the second chanting and the third mantra after the third chanting, when they have arrived at the place of disposal (see below, note 137).

[[P128]]

135 Compare for jihmám yajñásya *síro* haret AiĀ 3, 2, 4: jihmaśiras, "with a crooked head,” which is an inauspicious omen.

136 Here *satman* is used to express a bodily rebirth in heaven. Normally we find sátanu and *sanga* “with a body” and “with limbs.” Because in the previous sentence the “bodily form" of the disposed Pravargya is emphasized (with the words *satman* and sátanu) a translation like “pourvu d'un atman" ("endowed with an atman"), that is elsewhere defensible (Minard, *Enigmes* 2:171), is here impossible. The same can be said of the translation "of the same essence" Gonda, *Prajāpati* and *the Year*:25 note 33; and Minard, loc. cit.

137 All the priests and the sacrificer and his wife are going from the Prācīnavaṃsa to the place where the Pravargya implements will be disposed. The Prastotṛ starts the *tyagnāyiḥ* sāman three times (van Buitenen, Pravargya*:*126); when they depart, when they arrive and when they are halfway.

138 amṛta evaínam *práti sthāpayati*, "he (the Adhvaryu) places it on the immortal” refers to the vessel that is being placed on the plate of gold, but, because of the identification of the Pravargya vessel with the sacrificer, also to the sacrificer. From this second point of view we might translate: "he (the Adhvaryu) fixes him (the sacrificer) firmly in the world of immortality.” Cf. ŚB 12, 8, 1, 22: “Gold is non-dying (amṛtam), in non-dying ('immortality) they thus finally establish themselves" (tr. Gonda in the article "Pratisthā” in *Select.Studies* 2:363).

139 Most Śrauta Sūtras prescribe that the mantra *"(You* are) the *four-*comered navel of the cosmic *law"* (TĀ 4, 11, 4(9)), should accompany the touching of the Uttaravedi with the hand. Such an act would leave the identification of the navel with the Pravargya baseless. The act prescribed by the Śrauta Sūtra of the Vājasaneyins, Katyāyana, fits in better with this (Taittirīya) identification. It prescribes that the Pravargya vessel should be placed on the Uttaravedi in such a way that it touches its navel. (Cf. van Buitenen, Pravargya:129, note 1 on §8, where L. should be read as K(atyāyana).) "The four-cornered" of the mantra, in the TĀ Pravargya Brāhmaṇa explanation interpreted as iyám (S; uttaravedi or *bhūmi*), is interpreted as "he who shines yonder (the sun); for the quarters are his corners" in the SB (ŚB 14, 3, 1, 17). The Kaṭha-fragments we have, contain nothing about this mantra.

140 The rest of the mantra that accompanies the filling of the main Pravargya vessel with curds mixed with honey is (TĀ 4, 11, 4(12b-d)); téna *várdhasva cā* ca pyāyasva  
*vardhiṣimahi ca vayám* á *ca* pyāsiṣimáhi, *“*O Gharma, this is *your* food, *this is your filling;* through it, grow *and swell*. *May we also grow* and *may* we *swell*.”

141 This (TĀ 4, 11, 5(13)) and the following seven mantras are called Gandharva mantras. They are recited when the Pravargya lies disposed. According to Baudhāyana, the Adhvaryu has the sacrificer recite these mantras. Other Śrauta Sūtras have the mantras recited by all participants. The Gandharva is in Vedic texts the protector of Soma, of divine secrets and of the place of conception (Gonda, *Religionen* 1:100ff). He to *91*

[[P129]]

may have a solar character and be favourable to men, promulgating divine secrets and stimulating "visions” (RV 10, 177, 2; 10, 123; and below, 10, 139, 5; Gonda, Vision:91). "The Gandharva no doubt is the archetype of the poet who attempts to reveal the mystery" (Gonda, *Vision*:357). The Gandharva in the Ṛgveda often means the sun (RV 8, 77, 5; 9, 85, 12; 10, 123, *7)*, not only the physical sun, but also the spiritual sun that brings enlightenment and is situated or perceived in the heart (cf. RV 10, 177, 1-2). Besides these eight Gandharva mantras, there are severál other Pravargya mantras in which this name occurs, e.g. in RV 10, 123, 4; 5; 7 in the Hotṛ's recitation during fanning; and in RV 9, 83, 4 in the Hotṛ's recitation during the milk-offering.

142 *bandhu* or, with the suffix -tā creating abstract nouns, bandhútā, means “connection," "relation," "relative.” In Brāhmaṇical thought it may have the specialized meaning of the cosmic connection of some ritual element, or its "deeper sense." It is one of the central notions in Brāhmaṇical thought and is basic for many identifications. In fact many complete identifications (A=B) may actually be short expressions for: "there exists a *bándhu* (connection, relationship) between A and B.” If some sacrificial element or act is considered to have a special connection with some macrocosmic aspect or event, then this connection in the macrocosmic sphere may be called its *bándhu* or deeper sense. See Oldenberg, Weltanschauung⁴, 110-123; S. Schayer, “Struktur der magischen Welt-anschauung" in *Zeitschrift* *für* Buddhismus, 1925:259ff.; Renou in *JA* 1953:171-175; Gonda, “Bandhu in the Brāhmaṇas,” *ALB* 29 (1965):1-29; more recently Oguibenine: “*Bandhu* et *Dakṣinā*. Deux termes védiques illustrant le rapport entre le signifiant et le signifié” in *JA* 1983:263-275.

143 This mantra (TĀ 4, 11, 6(17a-d)) occurs also in TS 4, 7, 13, 1 and TB 3, 10, 4, 2: *cíd* asi samudrayoniḥ I índur *dákṣaḥ* sycná *ṛtāvā  
híranyapakṣaḥ* śakunó *bhuranyúḥ*  
*mahắnt sadhásthe* dhruvá á níṣattaḥ, “You are Thought*, with* the ocean *as* *birthplace*. You *are* the *skilful* *drop*, *the hawk that is in* season, the *golden*-*winged quivering bird, sitting large* and *firm in* the assembly." The accent shows that *samudra-yoniḥ* is a bahuvrihi. Van Buitenen's "thought, source of the floods" is therefore incorrect. The ocean is the heart as the seat of thoughts (Introduction §3.1, p. 14; and Geldner *RVübers* 1, note on RV 1, 159, 4; F. B. J. Kuiper, "The Bliss of Aša":125; Gonda, Vision:281). The terminology of this mantra (occurring only in YV texts) is close to that of the Soma-hymns of the RV.

144 This and the following two mantras (TĀ 4, 11, 7/8(18-20)), are with slight differen-ces RV 10, 139, 4-6. While these mantras were often interpreted in the light of the mythi-cal theft of the Soma by the Gandharva Viśvāvasu (e.g. K. Rönnow, *Trita Aptya,* Uppsala 1927:89; Geldner, *RVübers*, ad loc.), there is a lot to say for Lüders' hypothesis that they rather refer to the discovery of the sun by Indra (Lüders, *Varuṇa* 2:540; Renou, *ÉVP* 15:32, appears to prefer Lüders' interpretation). Soma is invoked in verse 4, but there is no reference to his being taken away. The whole sūkta RV 10, 139 is a hymn to the rising sun (Lüders, *Varuṇa* 2:538). This was probably also the way these verses were inter[[P130]]

preted by those who introduced them into this rite, because this interpretation fits the general character of the Pravargya and the use of the verses at this place in the rite much better than the Soma-theft interpretation advocated here by S. So, we may assume that these verses originally referred to the discovery of the sun by Indra, and were as such introduced in the rite. According to Lüders the Gandharva helps Indra in this matter. But it is very well possible that the sun was in this hymn actually identified with the Gan-dharva (RV 8, 77, 5 is also best interpreted in that way). Such an interpretation, at least, seems to have formed the reason for employing this verse as a mantra at this place of the rite. What remains a difficulty in the first verse, also in this interpretation, is *tát* and *āsām* in pāda c, metrically superfluous in the Ṛgveda. Did Indra follow the Rita (cosmic law) of the waters (Lüders)? Did he follow their track *from* the sun, or rather *towards* the sun?
