145 (a) The complete verse is (TĀ 4, 11, 8(20)): sásnim *avindac* cárane nadinām  
ápāvmod *dúro* áśmavrajānām  
*prāsām* gandharvó amṛtāni vocat  
indro *dákṣam* pāri *jānād ahinam*, *“*He *found the Winner (*the sun) in the course of *the (*heavenly) rivers. *He opened* the gates for *(the* waters) *that were* enclosed in rock. The *Gandharva proclaimed the* immortal (names*)* of the (waters). Indra became fully acquainted *with* (his) perfect *skill.”* TĀ 4, 11 has ahinam, easily construable with *dákṣam*, instead of ahīnām in RV 10, 139, 6, (genitive plural of the difficult ahí, for which see Lüders, Varuṇa 2:535).

(b) *āsām* (feminine genitive plural) are female entities enclosed in rock: either the cows (gấvas) or the waters *(*ápas).

146 In the context of the mantras it seems most logical to interpret amṛtāni (neuter plural) as “immortal names." On the basis of praná *vá amṛtāḥ* it is not clear whether the author of the TĀ did the same.

147 The same formulas are employed in BSS 2,5 (Krick, Feuergründung:72, note 185).

148 TĀ 4, 11, 8(24) has only the two *pratikas*. The complete verses are TS 1, 4, 43a (RV 1, 50, 1): *úd* u *tyám* jātávedasam *devám vahanti ketávaḥ i dṛśé víśvāya* sūryam, "His *rays bear upward* the god *Jātavedas*, *that all* may see *the* sun," and TS 1, 4, 43b (RV 1, 115, 1):citrám devānām úd *agād* ánikam *cákṣur* mitrásya váruṇasyāgnéḥ  
áprā dyāvāpṛthivi antarikṣam surya ātmā *jāgatas* tasthúṣaśca., *"Radiant has* risen the countenance of the *gods*, *the eye* of *Mitra, Varuṇa* and Agni. *He* has *filled* heaven and *earth* and *the* firmament. *The* sun *is the self* of *all* that moves *and* stays.”

149 Vi-with ablative with the meaning of Veyu-with ablative: "to be deprived of, lose.”

150 C: vaíinstead of *tvar*.

### Anuvāka 10

151 The different layers of imagery that are interwoven in this mythical passage may be distinguished as follows. To the usual identification of Prajāpati with the sun, a second identification with a cow is added. This cow is the genetrix or depositary of sacred

[[P131]]

knowledge in the form of speech and chant. Thus we have Prajāpati/sun/cow/depositary-of-sacred-knowledge. From him/t is obtained light (Sukra*)/*milk/sacred-knowledge-in-the-form-of-speech-and-chant. For the identification of Prajāpati with a cow cf. Gonda, *Prajāpati's* rise *to higher rank* (Leiden, 1986): 10-11. For the remarkable metaphor of the mantras and sāmans that are "milked" from a divine place or person, compare the following places in the RV. In RV 5, 44, 13 the seer Sutambhara is said to milk the udder of all *dhis*, *dhi* meaning vision or thought, but also its concrete manifestation in the form of mantra and song (cf. note 77 above), In RV 3, 57,

2 Indra and Pūṣan milk the everflowing (*sasaya*) [udder] of heaven. The preceding stanza makes it clear that the "everflowing sudder]” which is milked, is the vision (manisa*)* of the poet. In RV 3, 1, 9, Agni is said to have found the udder of the Father, and to have let flow from it dhara dhénā, nourishing streams of speech.

In our passage, the streams of inspired speech and songs, the Śukriya sāmans and yajus-formulas, arc first conceived in the form of undifferentiated *sukrám* páyas, “bright milk” or “something bright in the form of milk.” The brightness no doubt emphasizes the visionary character of this undifferentiated "stream" of sacred knowledge. In the RV, the word *sukra* is more than once found to characterize a word like *dhi:* a bright or pure shape or form *(Sukró* várṇaḥ) “belonged to the *dhiḥ* because of its very character as a vision, obtained by an extraordinary faculty of seeing and given to man by those gods who are particularly characterized by their relations to 'light'" (Gonda, *Vision*:167).

152 tád *agnir vy* akarot, the activity in relation to the undifferentiated “sāman- and yajus-milk," here expressed by the verb *vi*-kṛ, is very similar to the activity in relation to undifferentiated speech, expressed by vy-*ā*-kg, e.g. in TS 6, 4, 7, 3. See Thieme, "Meaning and form of the 'grammar' of Panini,” *Studien* zur Indologie und *Iranistik*, 8/9 (1982/3):23-28 for several examples with vy-d*-kr* in the context of speech. Thieme (loc. cit.) states the meaning of vy*-a-kṛ* to be among other things "to distinguish [by separating similar objects from one another or by separating a seeming whole into its parts]" and "to form in different ways." Whether or not these meanings are acceptable as the basis of the etymology of *vyakaraṇa,* they quite agree with the use of vy-*a-kṛ* in the context of speech, and with the use of *vi-kr* here in the context of the undifferentiated "sāman- and yajusmilk."

153 Śukriya is an old name for the Pravargya. In the so-called Arṣeyapatha of the Taitti-riyas, the *śukriyāṇi*, i.c. the Pravargya mantras and Brāhmaṇa, formed part of the second or Saumya kāṇḍa of this division of the Taittirīya Yajurveda (see Introduction section 5, p. 34f). While the study of these *śukriyāṇi* is connected with the Avāntaradīkṣā, the study of the Saumya kānda as a whole is connected with what is called the *śukriyavrata*. In the KaṭhĀ-manuscripts the term *Śukriya* is rather frequent (in its narrower sense of "Pravargya"); the santi recitation begins with *om,* Śukriyam *rudrasya,* the mantras are the *Śukriya-mantrāḥ* and the Pravargya Brāhmaṇa is called *śukriya-brāhmaṇam*.

[[P132]]

154 The imagery of Prajāpati/sun/cow/depositary-of-sacred-knowledge from whom/which light/milk/a-stream-of-sacred-knowledge is being obtained, is now connected with the ritual; the cow's and goat's milk are identified with the stream of sacred knowledge that is differentiated into the yajus-formulas and sāmans. Although this has not been explicitly stated, Prajāpati/sun/etc. is here no doubt identified with the Pravargya vessel.

155 Birds should not be allowed to sit around the disposed Pravargya until the next rite in the Agniṣṭoma: the carrying forth of the sacred fire. See Caland on ĀpŚS 15, 16, 7, where this passage of the TĀ has been translated. He adds: “Die Absicht ist nicht ganz klar” ("The intention is not completely clear"). *One* should compare KaṭhĀ 3, 186 and 239a. This last passage shows that "birds sitting around his children” means that the children have died; "Death aims at the children of him, at whose Mahāvīra, in the course of the Pravargya ceremony, birds or beasts of prey come running along. He should (in that case] offer two oblations with 'bloody-mouthed' and 'Go asunder (?)'." If the latter *pratīka* should be read as ví gā *indra* (Witzel, *KaṭhĀtransl*:138, note 186.2), the two verses occur also in TĀ 4, 29, 1(1) and 4, 28,1(1) (see van Buitenen, *Pravargya*, Appendix III, the Rites of Reparation, Ś20f.)

156 *upadika-*santatāḥ, not: "eaten up by white ants” (Rudradatta on ApŚS 15, 16, 5 and Śrautakośa*)* but "covered all over with white ants," For the connection of the upadika ants with water see above note 6.

157 *anujjhavari* may be derived from anu + ud + hā, with the suffix -vari. Caland (on ĀpŚS 15, 16, 5) hesitatingly: \*\*pflegt nach Wasser auszugehen', “ist wasserhaft”?” (“is in the habit of going out towards water', 'is watery"?"). He apparently had *hā, jihite* in mind. The other root, hā, *jahāti,* would result in a translation like "giving up, discharging water"; but it is the sun that is explained by the author as the collector and discharger of rain (cf. TĀ 5, 7, 7 and note 96 above). This sun is represented here by the Pravargya vessel, and the Darbha-grass is the place from which water is collected.

### Anuvāka 11

158 This series of identifications concerns the preparation, use and disposal of the vessel. The second series below shows the heating of the vessel and the offering of milk in its various stages.

159 The Mahāvīra is here the name of the last stage of the Pravargya. In the TĀ this name is not as frequent as in other texts (SB, KaṭhĀ) where it is often given to the Pravargya vessel. In the KaṭhĀ the sun is called Rudra Mahāvīra. Cf. note 52 of the Introduction.

[[P133]]

160 *púnyartim* tásmai kāmayate; cf. TB 3, 2, 3,7 *pradātré* púnyam asaste. The accentuation of *púṇyārtim* (on the first member of the compound) is in accordance with the regular accentuation in the older language of a compound with an action noun on -ti as the second member (Wackernagel-Debrunner, *Altindische* Grammatik*,* II (Göttingen 1957), 1:214 (§90a)). The usual translations of *ārti* are "painful occurrence, pain, injury, mischief; sickness" (e.g. Monier-Williams, *Sanskrit*-*English* Dictionary, P. 149; delete “for 2.ārti see s.v."). S and Bh interpret *árti* in this way (explaining *púnyārtim* as *bhagyahānim* (S) and *puṇyanāśam* (Bh)), and this creates two problems. First, the context forces them to attributé a special connotation to yathānāmám*.* S: “(merely) with his own given name" (*tadīyam* nārnānatikramya nāmakaraṇakāle *sampāditenaiva* nāmnā); Bh: "with a name not made for the (special) purpose” (*anarthakṛtena* nāmnā). More serious is the second problem: the person "who knows thus” (yá eyám *véda*) obtains something undesirable ("mischief or damage to his merit”), whereas the one "who knows thus" is elsewhere always rewarded with something desirable. Bh sensed the problem, and gave an alternative interpretation of *árti* as vyasana. He probably had in mind "arduous devotion to", one of the meanings of the latter word; but *árti* itself is nowhere else attested in that meaning. The solution proposed here is the following. The Vedic verb *ār* (*ā* + r) means "to reach, obtain", and is usually pregnant with a negative connotation ("to reach mischief"), but not always. Cf. for the latter case Grassmann, Wörterbuch zum Rig- Veda:99, under ar+a, and Sūryakānta, A Practical Vedic Dictionary: 145, under ār. The derivative action noun *árti* may have been used here in the neutral sense of “obtainment.” In the dictionaries it has been recorded only in the negative sense, which it has, it is true, in almost all cases: "obtainment of mischief: painful occurrence etc. " If *púnya* in *púnyartim* is adjectival, the translation is: "good, pure or meritorious obtainment.”

161 *jána* and *janátā*, collectiva in singular, may refer, according to Rau, *Staat:*64, to foreign, distant regions, to which one goes in order to obtain fame and wealth. Indian commentators, however, take the words in the meaning of "assembly" or "court-room” *(*janasamúha, *sabha*) here and elsewhere; cf. Caland, '*Wunschopfer*', 6 note 36.

162 See note 52 above.

163 Compare this and the following three identifications of the series, with the identifications of the Vaisvadeva Agnihotra in JB 1, 21 and parallel passages (Bodewitz, *JBtransl*:66ff.). We have here the same chronological order as in TB 2, 1, 7,1 (and VādhS 3,37) with *mārutáḥ* kváthan instead of *vaiśvadevá* bhindávaḥ (cf. MS 1, 8, 10: mārutám bindumát) and *maitráḥ* *Sárogrhitaḥ* instead of maitrám *sáraḥ*.

164 The earth is connected with the dead and with Untruth *(ánṛta)* and therefore clay as such would not be suitable for an oblation to the gods (see TB 3, 2, 3, 11 and Oldenberg, Die *Religion*:419). This is also the reason why one should not drink from a clay vessel during the study of the Pravargya mantras (TĀ 5, 8, 13), because the entire rite is

[[P134]]

directed towards the sun. The solution given here is that the vessel is "Speech," i.e. “it is of the nature of Speech because it is produced with mantras (and thus it is not an ordinary clay vessel)" (S). SB 14, 2, 2, 54 motivates the use of clay for the Pravargya vessel in a more down-to-earth way as follows: "But if it were made of wood, it would be burnt; and if of gold, it would dissolve; and if of copper, it would melt; and if of stone, it would burn the two handling sticks; and that (Gharma) itself submitted to that (earthen vessel): therefore, it is by means of an earthen (vessel) that he offers it.”

165 The basic identification underlying this rite is that of the Pravargya vessel/ceremony and the sun. Here the line of identifications continues in the well-known identifications of the sun, the Year and Prajāpati (See Gonda, *Prajāpati and the Year*:16, 90, 91).

166 For the Avakāśa mantras see note 79 above.

### Anuvāka 12

167 The identifications during the first five days can be commented upon as follows; at the beginning of sacrificial acts we usually find Savitṛ. Agni, Vāyu and Āditya (the sun) correspond to the three worlds: earth, firmament and heaven. The moon and the stars represent the fourth world. Cf. Bodewitz, "The Waters in Vedic Cosmic Classifications," Indologica Taurinensia*,* 10 (1982): 45-54.

168 Cf. TS 5, 7, 21 (on the Asvamedha), where the sixth offering is for the Year, the seventh for the Maruts, the eighth for Bṛhaspati, the ninth for Mitra, the tenth for Varuṇa, the eleventh for Indra, the twelfth for the All-gods. Elsewhere (TS 1, 7, 11) the seven-footed sakvari-metre is connected with the Maruts; Bṛhaspati and the gayatri-metre are on the eighth place; Mitra and the threefold stoma (consisting of nine verses) are on the ninth place; Varuṇa (in the RV also called samráj, Emperor) and the ten-syllabic virāj-metre on the tenth place; Indra and the tristubh on the eleventh place; and the All-gods and the jagati-metre on the twelfth place.

169 The Pravargya in the Agniṣṭoma is usually performed before the Upasad (Caland- Henry, Agniṣṭoma: §50-51, §56-57 etc.). Some Śrauta Sūtras mention as an alternative that the Upasad can be performed first (ĀpŚS 11, 2, 5, HirŚS 7, 4, VaikhŚS 13, 7). The author possibly refers to these two alternatives. However, the two properties "shining from here" and "shining from there" seem to belong to the Pravargya simultaneously. Does the author refer to two ways of looking at the same series of performances: Pravargya-Upasad, Pravargya-Upasad, Pravargya-Upasad, can be taken (Pravargya), Upasad-Pravargya, Upasad-Pravargya, Upasad-7 170 The radiation of heat (*aívá tapati*) by him “who knows thus" should of course be interpreted in the light of the concept of *tápas* (note 74). Bh comments on this final word of the Taittirīya Pravargya section: "The meaning is: he (ie. the performer of the Pravargya who knows the implications) becomes the sun.”

[[P135]]

## Appendix 1: The Place of Sacrifice

The Place of Sacrifice for a Pravargya that forms part of a Soma Sacrifice The regular performances of the Pravargya (TĀ 5, anuvāka 4-8) take all place in the Pracinavamsa, except that the Adhvaryu and his assistant leave the hut through one of the doors, in order to milk the cow and goat (see anuvāka 7, and note 81). The twice daily heating of the Pravargya vessel takes place on the western or *pravṛijaniya* mound. At the end of each performance, except at the time of the final disposal, the implements are put on the Samraḍ-asandi or Emperor's Throne. The Patni-sālā (Wife's Hall) is at the west of the Prācina-vamsa.

The previously prepared clay-pit, from which the clay is procured for the preparation of the implements, is at a certain distance east of the Āhavanīya. To the north of this place is the black antelope skin, spread out with its neck towards the east and the hairy side upwards (anuvāka 2). At the time of the preparation of the implements, the Mahāvedi (Great Altar Space), the Uttaravedi, the Sadas (Hall of Recitation) and Havirdhāna (Soma Hall) are not yet meted out and put up. The ingredients are mixed and the implements are formed out of the clay-mixture on a mound to the north or north-east of the place of sacrifice. The implements are baked in a fire-pit, dug to the east of the Gārhapatya fire (BSS: to the north of the hut).

The disposal of the implements after the last performance (anuvāka 9 and 10) normally takes place at the by now constructed Uttaravedi. The Sadas and the Havirdhana are put up afterwards.

The plan shows the Pracinavamsa where the main actions of the Pravargya take place, and some places that will play a role in the Soma sacrifice to which the Pravargya is the introductory part.

The Samraḍ-asandi, the Emperor's Throne for the Pravargya vessels, is according to some not east but west (HirŚS) or north (KātyāyanaŚS; optional in VaikhŚS) of the King's Throne or Rājāsandi.

The peg for the cow is south of the middle of the southern door, according to the Sūtras within the sight of the Hoty. The pin for the calf is

[[P136]]

*Appendix* 1: *Place of Sacrifice* south of the eastern door post of the southern door, so towards the east of the cow's peg. The pin for the she-goat is towards the south of the western door post of the southern door, so towards the west of the cow's peg. The one for the lamb is north of the peg for the she-goat. According to ĀpŚS, the pin for the she-goat is closer to the southern door than the cow's peg and the calf's pin.

The western or *pravṛñjaniya* mound is north of the Gārhapatya; the eastern or udvāsaniya mound is north of the Āhavanīya; the *ucchista* or cleaning-up mound, is in the north-eastern region (according to BSS in the northern section of the sacrificial hall), and has an outlet on the outside.

Gārh. Gārhapatya or Domestic Fire. Ahav. Āhavanīya or Offering Fire.

Daksin. Dakṣināgni or Southern Fire.

R.

S. m.

*kh*.

Utt. v. n.

Rājāsandi, the King's Throne for the Soma stalks.

Samraḍ-āsandi, the Emperor's Throne for the Pravargya vessels.

*methi* and *mayūkha*-s, the peg and pins for the cow, the calf, the she-goat, and the lamb.

*khara*-s, the mounds.

Uttaravedi. nābhi.

(The plan and the additional information are mainly based on: Planche IV of Caland- Henry, Agniṣṭoma (at the end of vol. 1); BSS 11, 6, 8-16; ĀpŚS 15, 6, 10-22; HirŚS 24, 2, 10-11; van Buitenen, Pravargya, Plate 2 (opp. to p. 12); Staal, *Agni* 1:376-377, Plate 60B.)

## Appendix 2: Concordance of the three editions

The Pravargya Mantras and Brāhmaṇa of the Taittirīya Āraṇyaka Concordance of the three editions C is the Calcutta edition, M the Mysore edition, P the Poona édition (see Abbreviations under TĀ). Prap. is the prapāṭhaka, An. the anuvāka, Da. the daśati; in the case of the Pravargya mantras, the last number (between parentheses), is the number a specific part of the text has in edition C or M. The letters a, b, c... are attributed by me to the pādas or portions between two dandas, if they have to be indicated specifically. "om" means that a certain part has been omitted or is absent. In case a number appears - by mistake - only in the notes and not in the text, the number has been marked with \*. C and P (both with S's commentary) are almost similar, except that P does not indicate the numbers of the different portions of the text. Other differences (which are very few) have been indicated between parentheses.
